\section{Introduction}\label{sec-intro}

Du, Ge and Qiao (2014) estimate how expectations about the renminbi
(RMB)--US dollar exchange rate affected the accumulation of China's
foreign exchange reserves (FER) between 2000 and 2011. They split the
sample into four exchange-rate regimes and measure expectations in two
ways: a forecast generated from a three-stage regression model (Model I)
and the 12-month non-deliverable forward rate (NDF, Model II).
\textbf{{[}YOUR READING: in two or three sentences, the article's
central claim as stated in its abstract and conclusion, quoted or
closely paraphrased, with page numbers.{]}}

The question matters because \textbf{{[}YOUR READING: why this relation
mattered, for example reserve accumulation and sterilisation costs,
``hot money'' inflows under an expected appreciation, and the policy
debate at the time. One paragraph, with two or three references.{]}}

This note is a replication in the sense of the \emph{Journal of Comments
and Replications in Economics}: it assesses the reliability of the
article's claims. We do two things. First, we \textbf{reproduce} the
published estimates from the data of the original document on which the
article is based, which contains the full dataset and every test and
estimation table (Section~\ref{sec-reproduction}). Second, we
\textbf{correct} twelve problems in the original analysis, one at a
time, and show how each correction changes the estimated effect of
expectations (Sections \ref{sec-corrections} and \ref{sec-effect}).

\textbf{Disclosure.} The author of this note is the first author of the
original article. To prevent the replication from being steered towards
a preferred result, every rule that selects data, specifications or
classifications was written down, dated, \emph{before} the estimates it
governs were computed, and the code and results of each step were
committed to git. These rules include which balance-of-payments series
is used, which unit-root specification, which Model I specification
counts as the reproduction, and when an estimate counts as reproduced.
Each rule is recorded, with the options considered and the evidence, in
a numbered decision log, which Appendix A lists. \textbf{{[}W1-09: the
log is kept off the public repository; state whether a cleaned version
is published with the package or is available on request.{]}} All code
and all public data are available at \textbf{{[}REPOSITORY URL{]}}; the
only inputs that cannot be redistributed are the original document and
the Wind NDF series (Section~\ref{sec-availability}). The co-authors of
the original study were informed of this replication on
\textbf{{[}DATE{]}}. \textbf{{[}CONFIRM OR EDIT{]}}

Section~\ref{sec-original} describes the original study,
Section~\ref{sec-data} the data, Section~\ref{sec-reproduction} the
reproduction, Section~\ref{sec-corrections} the corrections, and
Section~\ref{sec-effect} their effect on the conclusions.
Section~\ref{sec-availability} explains how to reproduce every result,
and Section~\ref{sec-conclusion} concludes.

\section{The original study}\label{sec-original}

\subsection{Models}\label{models}

The article estimates two models of the monthly change in reserves,
\(\Delta FER_t\), separately in four periods. \textbf{Model I} builds an
expected change of the exchange rate in three stages. Stage 1 regresses
the change of the exchange rate (RMB per USD) on fundamentals:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-stage1}{
\Delta ER_t = b_0 + b_1\,\Delta BOP_t + b_2\,\Delta i_t + b_3\,\Delta \pi_t + b_4\,\Delta GDP_t + x_t .
}\end{equation}

Stage 2 projects the stage-1 result on its own lags, without a constant:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-stage2}{
\widehat{\Delta ER}_t = \sum_{s=1}^{j} a_s\,\widehat{\Delta ER}_{t-s} + e_t ,
}\end{equation}

and stage 3 uses the fitted value \(\widehat{\widehat{\Delta ER}}_t\) as
the expectation:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-stage3}{
\Delta FER_t = \tau_0 + \tau_1\,\widehat{\widehat{\Delta ER}}_t + \tau_2\,\Delta NX_t + \tau_3\,\Delta FDB_t + \tau_4\,\Delta FDI_t + y_t .
}\end{equation}

\textbf{Model II} replaces the generated expectation by the observed
12-month NDF:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-model2}{
\Delta FER_t = g_0 + g_1\,\Delta NDF_t + g_2\,\Delta GDP_t + g_3\,\Delta NX_t + g_4\,\Delta FDB_t + g_5\,\Delta FDI_t + w_t .
}\end{equation}

The variables are defined in Table~\ref{tbl-variables}. In the original
document, the regressions of Periods 1 and 3 have AR(1) errors
(ARIMA(1,1,0)) and those of Periods 2 and 4 are estimated by OLS. The
periods follow the exchange-rate regime (Table~\ref{tbl-periods}).

\section{Data}\label{sec-data}

\subsection{The original data}\label{the-original-data}

All data and all reproduction targets are read by code from the original
document; nothing is transcribed by hand. Appendix I of the original
document supplies nine monthly variables for the 144 months from January
2000 to December 2011 (Table~\ref{tbl-variables}). The extracted data
were checked against the original study's descriptive statistics (Tables
3.2 to 3.9): of the 304 cells of those tables, 267 agree to the printed
decimals on the raw data (decision W2-13; tolerance: half a unit of the
last printed digit, W2-07). \textbf{{[}YOUR READING: how the remaining
cells are explained, from W2-13: for example the Period 1 correlations
involving the constant exchange rate.{]}}

\subsection{Inputs not in the original
appendix}\label{inputs-not-in-the-original-appendix}

Two regressors are not in the appendix and were collected anew, and
price data were added for the corrections (Table~\ref{tbl-sources};
State Administration of Foreign Exchange (2026); Wind Information Co.
(2026); International Monetary Fund (2026); Organisation for Economic
Co-operation and Development (2026); National Bureau of Statistics of
China (2026)).

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Balance of payments.} The quarterly current account (SAFE,
  BPM6 presentation) is converted to months. Of three candidate
  conversions (quarterly value held, divided by three, year-to-date),
  the one used, bop\_ca\_ytd, was chosen by a rule fixed before any
  Model I estimate. The rule combines the distance to the original
  unit-root statistic and to the four stage-1 Durbin--Watson statistics
  (W2-01, W2-10, W2-12).\footnote{The original document predates SAFE's
    BPM6 presentation and probably used BPM5 data, so a residual
    mismatch may reflect the accounting standard rather than the method.}
\item
  \textbf{NDF.} The 12-month USD/CNY NDF is the only Wind tenor that
  covers 2000--2011. Month-end and month-average versions of the daily
  series were compared with the original unit-root statistic before
  Model II was estimated; the version used is ndf\_mend (W2-06, W2-10).
\item
  \textbf{Consumer prices.} For the deflator correction
  (Section~\ref{sec-deflator}), a fixed-base consumer price index for
  China was taken from the IMF data portal and cross-checked against
  FRED (OECD data). The NBS published year-on-year index (same month of
  the previous year = 100) was used to identify the original study's
  inflation series (W4-05, W4-14).
\end{itemize}

\section{Reproduction}\label{sec-reproduction}

\subsection{Procedure}\label{procedure}

The reproduction follows rules fixed before estimation. Because the
original document does not fully document Model I, all twelve
combinations of its undocumented choices were estimated. These choices
are whether stage 2 projects the stage-1 fitted values or the observed
exchange rate, the number of lags (one to three), and whether the
variables are used as published or divided by \(\pi/100\), as the
article states. The combination that reproduces most published cells was
then named the headline (W3-02). Each estimate is classified on its sign
and star level only (W1-07), with the meanings of the marks taken from
the notes of the original tables and significance meaning \emph{p}
\textless{} 0.10. An estimate is \textbf{reproduced} if it has the same
sign and the same star level as the published one, or if both are
insignificant; \textbf{partially reproduced} if both are significant
with the same sign but a different star level; and \textbf{not
reproduced} if significance flips, or if the signs differ and either
estimate is significant. Magnitude, measured as the gap in published
standard errors, is reported but does not affect the class. First
differences are taken on the whole 2000--2011 series (the ``full''
convention, W3-01); differencing within each period is reported as a
sensitivity check.

\subsection{Unit-root tests}\label{unit-root-tests}

The article reports augmented Dickey--Fuller (ADF) tests; the original
tables report DF-GLS tests (Elliott et al. 1996). The original document
does not state its lag rule. The specification that best reproduces the
original statistics of the eight appendix variables was selected and
then fixed (W2-02, W2-09). Table~\ref{tbl-unitroot} shows the original
statistics, our DF-GLS statistics and the ADF statistics at the same
lag. DF-GLS and ADF agree on rejection at 5\% in 14 of 14 series.
\textbf{{[}YOUR READING: how close our DF-GLS statistics are to the
original study's (compare the first two columns), and whether any
stationarity judgement differs.{]}}

\textsuperscript{a} Original DF-GLS: original Tables 4.1 (levels) and

in Week 2 (deflated/trend/common/fixed\_1) and the same number of
lags.\\
\textsuperscript{b} Critical values at 5\%: DF-GLS from urca (Elliott,
Rothenberg and Stock 1996 tables), ADF from urca (Dickey-Fuller tables).
The original's critical values (Stata dfgls, finite-sample) differ by
construction; compare the statistics.\\
\textsuperscript{c} Rejects: the unit-root null is rejected at 5\%.

\subsection{Estimates}\label{estimates}

The headline Model I specification uses the alternative stage-2
regressor with 2 lag(s) on deflated data. It reproduces 19 of the 20
Model I cells; 0 are partially reproduced and 1 not reproduced. The
ranking among the 12 combinations was decided by count (W3-07). For
Model II on deflated data, 22 of 24 cells are reproduced (W3-08).
Because two insignificant estimates count as reproduced whatever their
size, the counts rest largely on such cells (W3-14): of the reproduced
Model I cells, 14 are insignificant in both the original and our
estimates, and 15 lie within one published standard error of the
published value; for Model II, 18 and 20. Tables \ref{tbl-repro-a} and
\ref{tbl-repro-b} (Tables 4.9 and 4.10 in the original document; Table 4
in the article) set the published and the reproduced estimates side by
side, and Figure~\ref{fig-gap} shows each gap in units of the published
standard error.

\textsuperscript{a} Published: original Table 4.9 as printed (marks as
printed); the numbers in parentheses are those printed below each
coefficient, which the article labels t-statistics (see the section
`Standard errors labelled as t-statistics').\\
\textsuperscript{b} Reproduced: our estimates, Model I with the
alternative stage-2 regressor and 2 lag(s) on deflated data (rank 1
under the pre-specified rule W3-02; result W3-07); Model II on the same
data; differences on the full convention. Standard errors in
parentheses: OPG for ARIMA periods, OLS otherwise.\\
\textsuperscript{c} n.i. = not identified: the regressor has no
variation in that period (the exchange rate was pegged in Period 1).\\
\textsuperscript{d} Estimator: ARIMA = regression in first differences
with AR(1) errors (ARIMA(1,1,0) in levels), exact maximum likelihood;
OLS otherwise.\\
\textsuperscript{e} Our coefficients are rounded to the decimals printed
in the original.\\
\textsuperscript{f} Shading of the reproduced estimates (classification
W1-07, on sign and star level only; significant = p \textless{} 0.10):
green = reproduced (same sign and same star level, or both
insignificant); yellow = partially reproduced (same sign, both
significant, different star level); red = not reproduced (significance
flips, signs differ with either significant, or not identified).\\
\textsuperscript{g} Significance (legend of original Tables 4.9-4.10): †
p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p
\textless{} 0.001 (two-sided; normal for the ARIMA periods, t for
OLS).\\
\textsuperscript{h} Periods: 1 = 2000-01 to 2005-06; 2 = 2005-07 to
2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

\renewcommand*{\arraystretch}{1}

\textsuperscript{a} Published: original Table 4.10 as printed (marks as
printed); the numbers in parentheses are those printed below each
coefficient, which the article labels t-statistics (see the section
`Standard errors labelled as t-statistics').\\
\textsuperscript{b} Reproduced: our estimates, Model I with the
alternative stage-2 regressor and 2 lag(s) on deflated data (rank 1
under the pre-specified rule W3-02; result W3-07); Model II on the same
data; differences on the full convention. Standard errors in
parentheses: OPG for ARIMA periods, OLS otherwise.\\
\textsuperscript{c} n.i. = not identified: the regressor has no
variation in that period (the exchange rate was pegged in Period 1).\\
\textsuperscript{d} Estimator: ARIMA = regression in first differences
with AR(1) errors (ARIMA(1,1,0) in levels), exact maximum likelihood;
OLS otherwise.\\
\textsuperscript{e} Our coefficients are rounded to the decimals printed
in the original.\\
\textsuperscript{f} Shading of the reproduced estimates (classification

significant, different star level); red = not reproduced (significance
flips, signs differ with either significant, or not identified).\\

p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p

OLS).\\

2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

\subsection{Autocorrelation tests}\label{autocorrelation-tests}

The original study tests residual autocorrelation with the
Durbin--Watson statistic and, where it is inconclusive, with the
Breusch--Godfrey test (original Tables 4.3 to 4.8). Over all tables,
data versions and conventions, 1 of 168 statistics agree with the
printed values to the printed decimals, and 124 of 168 test decisions
agree (W3-12). On deflated data under the primary convention, the
decisions agree in 37 of 42 cases. \textbf{{[}YOUR READING: what you
conclude from statistics that do not reproduce while most decisions
do.{]}}

\subsection{Chow tests}\label{chow-tests}

The original study reports Chow tests of equal coefficients in adjacent
periods (original Table 4.11). Table~\ref{tbl-chow} shows them next to
three recalculations. In every model and pair, the published \emph{F}
statistic exceeds the largest recalculated one by a factor of at least
\(10^{-7.8}\) (W3-13). The published degrees of freedom match those of
the same regressions estimated in levels, rather than in differences, in
11 of 12 comparisons. \textbf{{[}YOUR READING: what the
degrees-of-freedom match suggests about how the published statistics
were computed. Mark it as your interpretation.{]}}

covariance, F = W/q.

\section{Corrections}\label{sec-corrections}

We take the twelve problems listed in the replication plan one at a
time. For each, we state what the original did, why that is a problem,
what we do instead, and what changes. The data and model changes are
cumulative, and we assess their combined effect in
Section~\ref{sec-effect}.

\subsection{Standard errors labelled as
t-statistics}\label{standard-errors-labelled-as-t-statistics}

The article describes the numbers in parentheses below each coefficient
as t-statistics. The significance marks printed in the same table allow
a direct test. If the parentheses held t-statistics, the marks would
follow from those numbers; if they held standard errors, the marks would
follow from the ratio of coefficient to parenthesis. Read as standard
errors, the parentheses reproduce the printed marks in 42 of 44 cells
(95\%); read as t-statistics, in 22 (50\%). The cells where the
standard-error reading does not reproduce the marks are: Model I, Period
1, DER\_hathat; Model I, Period 4, DFDB. \textbf{{[}YOUR READING: what
the numbers in parentheses are, and whether the non-matching cells lie
near a significance threshold (corr\_relabel.csv, column
implied\_t\_if\_se).{]}} Throughout this note, the parentheses of the
published tables are treated as standard errors.

\subsection{Units: level coefficients and log
differences}\label{units-level-coefficients-and-log-differences}

All variables of \ref{eq-stage1} to \ref{eq-model2} are first
differences of \emph{levels}, so each coefficient is measured in the
units of the variables in Table~\ref{tbl-variables}, for example
reserves per unit change of the exchange rate. \textbf{{[}YOUR READING:
quote the article's percentage interpretation of a coefficient, with
page, and state why a level coefficient cannot be read as a
percentage.{]}} To give the coefficients a unit-free reading, all models
were re-estimated with log differences of the strictly positive series
(FER, the exchange rate, the NDF, GDP, FDB and FDI). The expectation
coefficient then is an elasticity; net exports, the balance of payments,
inflation and the interest rate stay in level differences (row 6 of
Tables \ref{tbl-steps-1} and \ref{tbl-steps-2}).

\subsection{Sign convention}\label{sign-convention}

Both expectation measures are quoted in RMB per US dollar, so a rise is
an expected \emph{depreciation} of the RMB. A positive coefficient
therefore means that reserves grow faster when a depreciation is
expected, and a negative one that they grow faster when an appreciation
is expected. \textbf{YOUR READING: how the article describes the sign in
its abstract and conclusion, and whether that description matches this
convention. Use the Period 4 Model II coefficient: published sign
from~\ref{tbl-repro-b}}

\subsection{Generated regressors}\label{generated-regressors}

In Model I, the stage-3 regressor is estimated in stages 1 and 2. The
conventional stage-3 standard errors treat it as data and so ignore the
sampling variation of the earlier stages (Pagan 1984; Murphy and Topel
1985). A bootstrap of the whole three-stage chain includes that
variation. Stages 1 to 3 are re-estimated in each of 999 replications,
with seed 20261001 (W4-01). The primary scheme is a wild bootstrap
(Mammen 1993; Davidson and Flachaire 2008): the residuals of stage 1 and
the innovations of stage 3 are multiplied by the same Rademacher draw in
each month, which preserves their correlation, and the AR(1) errors of
Periods 1 and 3 are rebuilt from the sign-flipped innovations. A
moving-block bootstrap within periods (Künsch 1989) is reported as a
check. Every coefficient was estimated in at least 999 of 999
replications.

For the headline specification on deflated data, the ratio of the
bootstrap to the conventional standard error of the expectation
coefficient is, by period: Period 1 0.55, Period 2 1.18, Period 3 1.02,
Period 4 0.79 (wild) and Period 1 1.65, Period 2 2.03, Period 3 1.10,
Period 4 2.35 (block). Figure~\ref{fig-ci} compares the intervals, and
Figure~\ref{fig-draws} shows the bootstrap distributions. \textbf{YOUR
READING: whether the conventional standard errors understate or
overstate the uncertainty, and whether any significance mark changes
(row 2 of~\ref{tbl-steps-1}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-ci}Expectation coefficient of Model I by period:
95\% intervals from the conventional standard error, the wild and the

\begin{figure}[H]

}

\subsection{Year-to-date GDP}\label{year-to-date-gdp}

In the appendix, the GDP of every month of a quarter is the cumulative
GDP of the year to date. Its first difference is therefore zero in two
months of each quarter, positive at each quarter start, and strongly
negative every January, when the cumulation restarts. This January fall
is an accounting artefact that enters both models as a regressor. The
correction replaces it by discrete quarterly GDP (the difference of
consecutive year-to-date values), held in each month of the quarter
(W4-05). The code first verifies that the appendix value is constant
within every quarter. The effect is shown in row 3 of
Table~\ref{tbl-steps-1} and Table~\ref{tbl-steps-2}, to be compared with
row 1b, the reproduction on the same (raw) data.

\subsection{Deflator}\label{sec-deflator}

The article divides every variable by \(\pi/100\). Here \(\pi\) is the
consumer price index with the same month of the previous year = 100, a
year-on-year growth factor \(P_t/P_{t-12}\) rather than a price level.
Dividing by it gives \(X_t P_{t-12}/P_t\): the real value multiplied by
last year's price level, not a real series. The correction divides the
value variables (FER, GDP, net exports, FDI, external debt and the
balance of payments) by a fixed-base consumer price index, rebased to
2000 = 100, from the IMF. The exchange rate and the NDF are not
deflated: they are prices of one currency in another, and a real
exchange rate would require both countries' price levels
(W4-05).\footnote{The US-dollar variables are thereby deflated by
  China's consumer prices, which measures their purchasing power over
  Chinese goods.}

We check the price data in two ways (Table~\ref{tbl-cpi} and
Figure~\ref{fig-cpi}). First, we compare the original \(\pi\) with the
same-month year-on-year index published by the National Bureau of
Statistics (NBS): 144 of 144 months agree within the printed decimals,
with a largest gap of 0.000 points (W4-14). \textbf{{[}YOUR READING:
what this comparison shows about the source of the original \(\pi\).{]}}
Second, the year-on-year rates implied by the IMF index agree with the
published ones within the printed decimals in 31 of 144 months (largest
gap 0.592 points).\footnote{We compared the FRED series with the IMF's
  in the same way (largest year-on-year gap 0.0000). \textbf{{[}YOUR
  READING: whether FRED is an independent measure or the same index, and
  therefore what it confirms.{]}}} \textbf{{[}YOUR READING: from
Figure~\ref{fig-cpi}, where the gaps between the published and the
index-implied rates lie in time. ``Our interpretation suggests
\ldots{}'' (for example the NBS basket changes; mark as a
hypothesis).{]}}

\subsection{Period 1: exchange-rate
variation}\label{period-1-exchange-rate-variation}

From January 2000 to June 2005 the RMB was pegged to the US dollar, and
the appendix exchange rate is constant (standard deviation 0.000 on raw
data). Its change is zero, so no expectation effect can be estimated
from it: the coefficient is not identified (W3-10). In the article's
deflated data the exchange rate varies (standard deviation 0.138), and
that variation comes from the deflator: the \(R^2\) of the deflated
exchange-rate change on the change of \(\pi\) is 0.999
(Table~\ref{tbl-period1}). A Period 1 ``expectation effect'' estimated
on deflated data is therefore an effect of inflation. With the exchange
rate undeflated (the corrected data), the Period 1 coefficient is
reported as not identified.

\subsection{Unit-root test label}\label{unit-root-test-label}

The article reports ADF tests; the original study ran DF-GLS tests.
Table~\ref{tbl-unitroot} reports both at the same lag, and they agree on
rejection in 14 of 14 series. This correction relabels the tests and
changes no conclusion that depends on them. \textbf{CONFIRM
from~\ref{tbl-unitroot}}

\subsection{Chow tests, correctly
specified}\label{chow-tests-correctly-specified}

The classic Chow test (Chow 1960) assumes equal error variances in the
two regimes and no autocorrelation. Both assumptions are doubtful with
monthly data across exchange-rate regimes. The corrected test estimates
one regression over 2000--2011 in which every coefficient is
regime-specific (Section~\ref{sec-pooled}), and tests the equality of
all coefficients of two adjacent regimes with a Wald test based on
Newey--West standard errors (last column of Table~\ref{tbl-chow};
Table~\ref{tbl-pooled}). For the corrected data, Model I, the tests
give: regimes 1--2, \emph{F}(4, 122) = 7.84, \emph{p} \textless{} 0.001;
regimes 2--3, \emph{F}(5, 122) = 4.52, \emph{p} \textless{} 0.001;
regimes 3--4, \emph{F}(5, 122) = 0.80, \emph{p} = 0.553. For Model II,
in the same order: \emph{F}(6, 119) = 6.50, \emph{p} \textless{} 0.001;
\emph{F}(6, 119) = 12.91, \emph{p} \textless{} 0.001; \emph{F}(6, 119) =
3.97, \emph{p} = 0.001. \textbf{{[}YOUR READING: whether the regime
split of the original is supported, pair by pair.{]}}

\subsection{Period 2: pooled model with regime
interactions}\label{sec-pooled}

Period 2 has about a year of monthly observations and five or six
regressors, leaving very few residual degrees of freedom. Following the
replication plan, the four periods are pooled in one regression with
regime-specific constants. In the \emph{full} variant every coefficient
is regime-specific, which gives the same point estimates as the separate
regressions but one set of standard errors for the whole sample. In the
\emph{expectation-only} variant only the constant and the expectation
term are regime-specific and the controls are common (W4-02). Standard
errors are Newey--West, with a lag of 4 (Newey and West 1987, 1994)
(W4-03).

The expectation-only variant is valid only if the controls have the same
effect in every regime. The Wald test of that restriction in the full
model gives \emph{F}(9, 122) = 3.76, \emph{p} \textless{} 0.001 for
Model I and \emph{F}(12, 119) = 3.55, \emph{p} \textless{} 0.001 for
Model II (corrected data). The test that the expectation coefficient is
the same in all regimes gives \emph{F}(2, 122) = 3.33, \emph{p} = 0.039
(Model I) and \emph{F}(3, 119) = 1.55, \emph{p} = 0.206 (Model II).
\textbf{{[}YOUR READING: whether the equal-controls restriction is
rejected, and therefore which of the two pooled variants is the
appropriate one; then what the pooled model says about Period 2.{]}}

\textsuperscript{a} Pooled OLS over 2000-2011, data version `corrected',
level form, with regime-specific constants. full: every coefficient
regime-specific (point estimates equal the separate per-period OLS fits;
controls not shown); exp\_only: only the constant and the expectation
term regime-specific, controls common.\\
\textsuperscript{b} Newey-West standard errors in parentheses: Bartlett
kernel, lag floor(4(n/100)\^{}(2/9)), no prewhitening, small-sample
factor n/(n-k); p-values from t(n-k). Results with the automatic
bandwidth are in corr\_pooled\_coefs.csv and corr\_pooled\_tests.csv.\\
\textsuperscript{c} Tests: Wald tests with the Newey-West covariance,
reported as F = W/q with (q, n-k) degrees of freedom. A term not
identified in a regime is left out of the restrictions.\\
\textsuperscript{d} Model I uses the stage-3 expected change of the
exchange rate from the headline specification; its pooled standard
errors do not account for the generated regressor (see Table 3 for the
bootstrap).\\
\textsuperscript{e} Significance (legend of original Tables 4.9-4.10): †
p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p
\textless{} 0.001 (two-sided; normal for the ARIMA periods, t for
OLS).\\
\textsuperscript{f} Periods: 1 = 2000-01 to 2005-06; 2 = 2005-07 to
2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

\subsection{Interest-rate label}\label{interest-rate-label}

The article calls the interest rate ``real''. The appendix series is the
nominal one-year deposit rate; Table~\ref{tbl-variables} uses the
correct label. The label does not affect any estimate.

\subsection{NDF source}\label{ndf-source}

The article does not state the source of its NDF series. We use the
12-month USD/CNY NDF from Wind, identified by its Wind code in
Table~\ref{tbl-sources} (W2-06).\footnote{Wind lists the series as
  compiled from news reports; its 1-, 3- and 6-month tenors start only
  at the end of 2008, so the 12-month tenor is the only one covering the
  sample.} \textbf{{[}CONFIRM against W1-03 and W2-06.{]}}

\subsection{Corrected estimates}\label{corrected-estimates}

Table~\ref{tbl-corrected} reports both models on the corrected data
(discrete quarterly GDP, fixed-base CPI, exchange rates undeflated) with
the original study's estimators. For Model I it also shows the
wild-bootstrap standard error of the expectation term.

\textsuperscript{a} Data version `corrected': discrete quarterly GDP;
FER, GDP, NX, FDI, FDB and BOP divided by the IMF consumer price index
(2000 = 100); exchange rates, inflation and the interest rate not
deflated.\\
\textsuperscript{b} Model I: alternative stage-2 regressor, 2 lag(s)
(the headline specification); same estimators as the original
(ARIMA(1,0,0) by ML in Periods 1 and 3, OLS in Periods 2 and 4).\\
\textsuperscript{c} Second line: conventional standard error (OPG in the
ARIMA periods). Third line, expectation term of Model I only:
wild-bootstrap standard error of the whole three-stage chain (999
replications), marks from the centred bootstrap p-value.\\
\textsuperscript{d} n.i. = not identified (no variation of the exchange
rate under the Period 1 peg when it is not deflated).\\
\textsuperscript{e} Significance (legend of original Tables 4.9-4.10): †
p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p
\textless{} 0.001 (two-sided; normal for the ARIMA periods, t for
OLS).\\
\textsuperscript{f} Periods: 1 = 2000-01 to 2005-06; 2 = 2005-07 to
2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

\section{Effect on the conclusions}\label{sec-effect}

Tables \ref{tbl-steps-1} (Model I) and \ref{tbl-steps-2} (Model II)
follow the expectation coefficient through the corrections, one step at
a time. Row 0 is the published value, row 1 our reproduction, and row 1b
the same on raw data. Rows 3 to 6 each add one correction to the row
before, with the comparisons 1 → 1b (the article's deflation), 1b → 3
(GDP), 3 → 4 (fixed-base CPI) and 4 → 6 (log differences). Rows 2 and 5
replace the conventional standard errors by bootstrap ones, and row 7 is
the pooled model.

\textsuperscript{a} Coefficient on the stage-3 expected change of the
exchange rate (Model I) in each period (step 7: in each regime of the
pooled model), standard error below in parentheses.\\
\textsuperscript{b} Step 0: original, with the numbers in parentheses
read as standard errors. Step 1: our reproduction (headline data
version). Step 1b: the same on raw data. Steps 3-6 add one correction at
a time (README\_week4 s.2.1): 1b to 3 isolates the GDP correction, 3 to
4 the fixed-base CPI.\\
\textsuperscript{c} Steps 2 and 5: bootstrap standard error of the whole
three-stage chain (999 replications; wild: Rademacher; block: moving
blocks within periods); marks from the centred bootstrap p-value.\\
\textsuperscript{d} Step 6: coefficients are elasticities (log
differences of FER and of the exchange-rate terms). Step 7: pooled OLS
2000-2011 with regime-specific constants and expectation terms; full =
all coefficients regime-specific; exp\_only = controls common;
Newey-West standard errors.\\
\textsuperscript{e} n.i. = not identified.\\
\textsuperscript{f} Significance (legend of original Tables 4.9-4.10): †
p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p
\textless{} 0.001 (two-sided; normal for the ARIMA periods, t for
OLS).\\
\textsuperscript{g} Periods: 1 = 2000-01 to 2005-06; 2 = 2005-07 to
2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

\textsuperscript{a} Coefficient on DNDF (Model II) in each period (step
7: in each regime of the pooled model), standard error below in
parentheses.\\
\textsuperscript{b} Step 0: original, with the numbers in parentheses
read as standard errors. Step 1: our reproduction (headline data
version). Step 1b: the same on raw data. Steps 3-6 add one correction at
a time (README\_week4 s.2.1): 1b to 3 isolates the GDP correction, 3 to
4 the fixed-base CPI.\\
\textsuperscript{c} No bootstrap for Model II: its expectation regressor
is observed, not generated.\\
\textsuperscript{d} Step 6: coefficients are elasticities (log
differences of FER and of the exchange-rate terms). Step 7: pooled OLS
2000-2011 with regime-specific constants and expectation terms; full =
all coefficients regime-specific; exp\_only = controls common;
Newey-West standard errors.\\
\textsuperscript{e} n.i. = not identified.\\
\textsuperscript{f} Significance (legend of original Tables 4.9-4.10): †
p \textless{} 0.1, * p \textless{} 0.05, ** p \textless{} 0.01, *** p
\textless{} 0.001 (two-sided; normal for the ARIMA periods, t for
OLS).\\
\textsuperscript{g} Periods: 1 = 2000-01 to 2005-06; 2 = 2005-07 to
2006-05; 3 = 2006-06 to 2008-05; 4 = 2008-06 to 2011-12 (Replication
Plan s.3.3).

For reference, the Model II coefficient of Period 4 is -1,294.45* (SE
531.20) as published, -1,282.99* (SE 523.64) in our reproduction,
-1,506.26** (SE 482.76) on the corrected data, and -0.460** (SE 0.162)
as an elasticity. For Model I, Period 3, it is -200.07 (SE 476.61) as
published, 462.55 (SE 1,016.05) reproduced, and -2,871.44 (SE 2,699.49)
corrected, with a wild-bootstrap standard error of 1,558.61 (\emph{p} =
0.318).

(Period 1). Use the JCRE wording for interpretations, ``Our

\section{Reproducibility and data availability}\label{sec-availability}

Every result in this note is produced by the code in the replication
package, \textbf{{[}REPOSITORY URL{]}}, at commit 2447209, with R
version 4.5.3 (2026-03-11 ucrt) and the package versions recorded in
\texttt{renv.lock}; the tables and figures were generated on 2026-10-06.
The code starts from the raw files and runs in a fixed order; each
script writes a run log. The bootstrap uses a fixed seed, so reruns give
identical draws. \textbf{{[}CONFIRM from two 08\_corrections logs: a
rerun reproduced every estimate and bootstrap standard
error.{]}}\footnote{Python collects and registers the data; R estimates.
  Each script writes a run log to \texttt{outputs/logs/}.}

Public data (SAFE, IMF, FRED, NBS) are included in the package with
their download dates and checksums (Table~\ref{tbl-sources}). Two inputs
cannot be redistributed. The original document is unpublished, and its
reproduction targets (the numbers of its tables) are \textbf{{[}included
/ withheld; W4-08{]}}. The Wind NDF series is proprietary: a reader with
Wind access can export it using the code in Table~\ref{tbl-sources} and
run the package unchanged. Without it, everything except Model II and
the NDF unit-root test can be rerun. \textbf{{[}CONFIRM OR EDIT{]}}

\section{Conclusion}\label{sec-conclusion}

\textbf{{[}YOUR READING, one paragraph each, in JCRE's four-part logic:
(1) what was reproduced and what was not
(Section~\ref{sec-reproduction}); (2) which errors were found and
corrected (Section~\ref{sec-corrections}); (3) which of the article's
conclusions are confirmed and which are not (Section~\ref{sec-effect});
(4) what remains open, for example the Period 1 and Period 3 constants
and the Chow statistics.{]}}
